Sharp regularity near the grazing set for kinetic Fokker-Planck equations
Kyeongbae Kim, Marvin Weidner

Abstract
. We prove optimal regularity results for solutions to linear kinetic Fokker-Planck equations in bounded domains. Our contributions are two-fold. First, we establish the sharp C 1 / 2 C^{1/2} regularity for either diffuse reflection or prescribed in-flow boundary conditions. Previously, in this setting, it was only known that solutions are C α C^{\alpha} for some small α > 0 \alpha>0 . Second, we provide a complete characterization of the solution behavior near the grazing set by proving higher order expansions beyond the critical regularity threshold of 1 2 \frac{1}{2} . These results demonstrate for the first time that solutions maintain higher smoothness up to the grazing set near the incoming boundary.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q84, 35B65, 82C40

1.
Introduction

The goal of this article is to investigate the boundary behavior of solutions to linear kinetic equations in domains, subject to physical boundary conditions. The prototype model of our analysis is the classical Kolmogorov equation

(1.1)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\Delta_{v}f=F\quad\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n},

yet all our results hold for the general class of kinetic Fokker-Planck equations given by

(1.2)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F\quad\text{in }(0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

with sufficiently smooth coefficients. These are well-known models in kinetic theory, arising naturally as the linearization of the Landau equation from plasma physics.

Establishing the regularity of solutions is a central challenge in kinetic theory. In the context of nonlinear models like the non-cutoff Boltzmann and Landau equations, regularity is directly linked to well-posedness
[
19
,
16
,
14
,
18
,
25
]
and convergence to equilibrium
[
5
,
6
]
.
In recent years there has been huge interest in the regularity of linear kinetic equations such as (
1.2
)
[
12
,
24
]
and their nonlocal counterparts
[
26
,
27
]
, since these results provide the necessary estimates to establish conditional regularity results for the Boltzmann and Landau equations
[
4
,
12
,
19
]
,
[
26
,
28
,
10
]
. Yet, despite these advances, the theory remains largely limited to interior regularity results or to
x
-periodic solutions.

Characterizing the behavior of solutions near physical boundaries remains an outstanding challenge in kinetic theory that is currently far from understood, even for the Kolmogorov equation (
1.1
), which is the simplest canonical model. The difficulty is largely due to the characteristic nature of the boundary, which leads to singular solution behavior near the grazing set
[
13
,
23
]

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}=(0,T)\times\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:v\cdot n_{x}=0\}.

To date, a complete regularity theory is only available for solutions with specular reflection boundary condition
[
35
]
, namely

(1.3)

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)=f(t,x,R_{x}v)\quad\text{for }x\in\partial\Omega,\quad\text{where }R_{x}v=v-2(v\cdot n_{x})n_{x},

where
n_{x}
denotes the outward unit normal vector at
x
. In
[
35
]
, it was proved that solutions are at most
C^{4,1}
up to
\partial\Omega
and that this regularity is optimal as
(t,x,v)
approaches the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
, whereas solutions are
C^{\infty}
away from
\gamma_{0}
. Previously, it was only known that solutions are
L^{\infty}_{t}C^{\alpha/3,\alpha}_{x,v}
and
C^{1,\alpha}_{v}
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)

[
8
,
15
]
.

Beyond this, other physically relevant boundary conditions have remained largely unexplored, and available results fail to reach the optimal regularity threshold, as we will discuss below.

The first main contribution of this article is to resolve this open question by establishing
optimal regularity estimates
for solutions with either
diffuse reflection
boundary condition or prescribed
in-flow
. Both of these conditions induce a boundary behavior that stands in stark contrast to the one of specular reflection, since solutions are in general not better than
C^{1/2}
up to the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
.

Second, motivated by the singular behavior of solutions near the grazing set, we establish
higher order expansions at
\gamma_{0}
that surpass the regularity threshold of
\frac{1}{2}
. This provides a precise characterization of the asymptotic behavior of solutions at higher order. As an application, we establish the following properties of solutions
f
to (
1.2
) with zero in-flow (i.e. boundaries are absorbing), which, to the best of our knowledge, are the first regularity results
above the critical threshold of
\frac{1}{2}
:

•

near the incoming boundary,
f\in C^{3-\epsilon}
up to
\gamma_{0}
, i.e. as
v\cdot n_{x}\nearrow 0
,
f
is better than
C^{1/2}
,

•

away from the incoming boundary,
f/\Phi\in C^{0,1}
up to
\gamma_{0}
for an explicit function
\Phi\in C^{1/2}
.

In the sequel, we will explain our main results in detail and discuss their novelty.

1.1.
Optimal regularity estimates

We decompose the kinetic boundary
\gamma:=(0,T)\times\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
into its outgoing, incoming, and grazing parts
\gamma=\gamma_{+}\cup\gamma_{-}\cup\gamma_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\gamma_{\pm}:=(0,T)\times\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\pm v\cdot n_{x}>0\},\quad\gamma_{0}:=(0,T)\times\{(x,v)\in\partial\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:v\cdot n_{x}=0\}.

There are several natural boundary conditions for kinetic equations (see
[
38
]
). The specular reflection boundary condition (
1.3
) has a clear physical interpretation and is mathematically tractable as it resembles a Neumann condition. However, since boundaries are usually rough on a microscopic scale, particles do not bounce elastically, but interact with the boundary and are re-emitted according to a velocity distribution determined by the boundary temperature
[
33
]
.

This mechanism is encoded in the diffuse reflection boundary condition, which is defined as follows, given a function
\mathcal{M}
1
1

1

Usually, one assumes
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{M}(t,x,w)(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}\textnormal{\,d}w=1
. A canonical choice would be
\mathcal{M}(v)=2e^{-|v|^{2}}
.
:

(1.4)

\displaystyle f=\mathcal{N}f\quad\text{in }\gamma_{-},\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{N}f(t,x,v)=\mathcal{M}(t,x,v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(t,x,w)(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}\textnormal{\,d}w.

Our first main result gives a complete characterization of the smoothness of solutions to (
1.2
) subject to the diffuse reflection boundary condition.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain and let
A,B,F\in C^{\infty}((0,T)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
A
be uniformly elliptic. Let
\mathcal{M}\in C^{\infty}(\gamma_{-})
. Let
f
be a weak solution to (
1.2
) with diffuse reflection condition (
1.4
),
and assume that
f,F
, and
\mathcal{M}
have fast decay
2
2

2

This means that
(1+|v_{0}|)^{k}f
,
(1+|v_{0}|)^{k}F
, and
(1+|v_{0}|)^{k}\partial^{\beta}\mathcal{M}
are bounded for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any multi-index
\beta
.
as
|v|\to\infty
.

Then, for any
[t_{0},t_{1}]\in(0,T)
, it holds

(i)

f
is smooth away from the grazing set, i.e.
f\in C^{\infty}(([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus\gamma_{0})
,

(ii)

f\in C^{1/2}([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\qquad\|f\|_{C^{1/2}([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\big(\|(1+|v|)^{q}f\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|(1+|v|)^{q}F\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big),

where
q,C>0
depend only on
n,t_{0},t_{1},\Omega,A,B,\mathcal{M}
.

As the following remark clarifies,
Theorem 1.1
is a simplified version of our main result on the diffuse reflection condition.

Remark 1.2
.

We actually show the following more general statements:

•

(ii) remains true when
\partial\Omega\in C^{2,\epsilon}
,
A\in C^{\epsilon}
for some
\epsilon>0
and
B,F\in L^{\infty}
(see
Theorem 8.2
).

•

We prove Schauder-type estimates of order
k+\alpha
away from
\gamma_{0}
in localized kinetic cylinders under suitable assumptions on the coefficients
A,B,F,\mathcal{M}
(see
Theorem 7.1
(ii)).

The
C^{1/2}
regularity is already new for solutions to the Kolmogorov equation (
1.1
), and it is sharp in the sense that we construct an explicit solution
f
to (
1.1
) in the half-space with
h,\mathcal{M}\in C^{\infty}
and such that
f\in C^{1/2}
, but
f\not\in C^{1/2+\epsilon}
for every
\epsilon>0
(see
subsection 7.2
). Moreover, note that also the
C^{\infty}
regularity away from
\gamma_{0}
is highly non-trivial due to the nonlocal dependence of the boundary data on the solution itself. We refer to Section
2
for the precise definition of the kinetic Hölder spaces.

Prior to our result, it was only known that solutions to (
1.2
) with diffuse reflection condition (
1.4
) are
C^{\alpha}
up to the boundary for some small
\alpha>0
(see
[
39
]
), both at
\gamma_{0}
and at
\gamma_{-}
. Proving regularity results up to the optimal order requires completely different techniques compared to
[
39
]
. Moreover, unlike specular reflection, (
1.4
) does not resemble a Neumann boundary condition. Hence, we cannot use the approach from
[
35
]
, and significant new ideas are required, as we explain below.

Instead, in this article we demonstrate that diffuse reflection shares the same singular boundary behavior of solutions near the grazing set
\gamma_{0}
as the in-flow boundary condition

(1.5)

\displaystyle f=g\quad\text{in }\gamma_{-},

where regularity is limited to
C^{1/2}
. Indeed, our second main result establishes the optimal
C^{1/2}
regularity for prescribed in-flow from which we ultimately derive
Theorem 1.1
by setting
g=\mathcal{N}f
. The in-flow condition, which prescribes the distribution of particles with incoming velocities, is another fundamental and physically relevant boundary condition in kinetic theory (see
[
38
]
).

The following theorem characterizes the boundary regularity of solutions to (
1.2
) with prescribed in-flow and it is the second main result of this article.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain and let
A,B,F\in C^{\infty}((0,T)\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
A
be uniformly elliptic. Let
g\in C^{\infty}(\gamma_{-})
. Let
f
be a weak solution to (
1.2
) with in-flow condition (
1.4
),
and assume that
f,g
, and
F
have fast decay as
|v|\to\infty
.

Then, for any
[t_{0},t_{1}]\in(0,T)
, it holds

(i)

f
is smooth away from the grazing set, i.e.
f\in C^{\infty}(([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus\gamma_{0})
,

(ii)

f\in C^{1/2}([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{1/2}([t_{0},t_{1}]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\left(\|(1+|v|)^{q}f\|_{L^{1}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}+[g]_{C^{1/2+\epsilon}_{q}(\gamma_{-})}+\|(1+|v|)^{q}F\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right),

where
q,C>0
depend only on
n,t_{0},t_{1},\Omega,A,B
.
3
3

3

We write
[g]_{C^{\alpha}_{q}(\gamma_{-})}=\sup_{z_{0}\in\gamma_{-}}(1+|v_{0}|)^{q}[g]_{C^{\alpha}(\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})\cap\gamma_{-})}
. See (
1.9
) for the definition of
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})
.

Remark 1.4
.

We comment on more precise versions of
Theorem 1.3
.

•

(ii) remains true when
\partial\Omega\in C^{2,\epsilon}
,
A\in C^{\epsilon}
, and
g\in C^{1/2+\epsilon}
for some
\epsilon>0
, and
B,F\in L^{\infty}
(see
Theorem 8.1
). We expect there to be non
C^{1/2}
solutions if
g\in C^{1/2}\setminus C^{1/2+\epsilon}
.

•

We also establish localized
C^{1/2}
estimates up to
\gamma_{0}
for solutions in the intersection of
(-1,1)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a kinetic cylinder (see
Theorem 6.2
).

•

Schauder-type estimates of order
k+\alpha
away from
\gamma_{0}
have already been established in
[
35
]
.

The
C^{1/2}
regularity up to the grazing set is already new for the Kolmogorov equation (
1.1
). Prior to our result, as for diffuse reflection, it was only known that solutions are
C^{\alpha}
for some small
\alpha>0
in case
g\not\equiv 0
(see
[
36
,
39
]
). Moreover, in the literature, there are several results on the boundary regularity for absorbing boundary conditions, i.e.
g\equiv 0
. Let us comment on these results and on how
Theorem 1.3
extends them:

Remark 1.5
.

•

In
[
23
,
21
,
22
]
, the authors prove that
f\in C^{1/2-\epsilon}_{v}\cap C^{1/6-\epsilon}_{x}
for any
\epsilon>0
up to the grazing set. Their results do not establish any regularity in
t
and they work with Hölder spaces that do not capture the transport structure of (
1.1
), compared to
Theorem 1.3
.

•

[
40
]
establishes
a priori

C^{1/2}
estimates for solutions. This means that
C^{1/2}
regularity of solutions up to the boundary has to be
assumed
in order for estimate to hold (see the absorption argument in
[
40
, Proposition 4.3, Lemma 4.7]
). In all our results, we merely assume
f
to be a weak solution, i.e.
f\in H^{1}_{v}
up to the boundary.

•

The results in
[
40
]
do not yield global estimates in
v
for global solutions to (
1.1
), such as
Theorem 1.3
, since the
|z_{0}|
-dependence of the constants is not explicit.

Moreover, we refer to
[
31
,
2
,
3
,
20
]
for further results on variational methods and trace theorems for kinetic equations with in-flow boundary conditions (
1.5
).

The sharpness of our result
Theorem 1.3
follows from well-known explicit
C^{1/2}
solutions to (
1.1
) with
g\equiv 0
in 1D, which go back to work by probabilists on first passage times of integrated Brownian motion from the last century (see
[
13
]
, and also
[
32
,
11
,
37
,
29
]
, and the more recent articles
[
23
,
39
]
).

Finally, while our results are new already for absorbing boundaries, extending these optimal regularity estimates to general non-zero in-flow data is a non-trivial task. This is mainly because standard barrier arguments do not seem to apply so easily in the kinetic setting. We refer to Subsection
1.3
for a more detailed discussion of our approach.

1.2.
Higher order estimates near the grazing set

The low
C^{1/2}
regularity of solutions at the grazing set stands in stark contrast to the smoothness of solutions near all other boundary points. This distinct behavior at
\gamma_{0}
arises because the transport operator degenerates for velocities tangent to the boundary, which breaks the hypoelliptic regularization of solutions. This phenomenon motivates a detailed analysis of the exact solution behavior near
\gamma_{0}
. In this context, the following question arises naturally:

\displaystyle\textit{Can we characterize the higher order asymptotics of solutions near }\gamma_{0}?

It is another major goal of this article to resolve this question. Moreover, it turns out that higher order asymptotics are crucial even in order to prove the
C^{1/2}
regularity, as we will explain below.

As a starting point of our analysis, we recall the explicit solution

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,v)=\begin{cases}x^{1/6}U\left(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v<0,\\
c_{0}x^{1/6}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}U\left(\frac{5}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v\geq 0\end{cases}

to the stationary Kolmogorov equation in 1D with absorbing boundaries

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}\phi_{0}&=\partial_{vv}\phi_{0}\qquad&&\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},\\
\phi_{0}&=0&&\text{on }\{0\}\times(0,\infty),\end{aligned}\right.

which goes back to
[
11
,
13
]
. Here,
c_{0}=\frac{\Gamma(7/6)}{\Gamma(1/6)}
and
U
denotes the Tricomi confluent hyper-geometric function. The precise boundary behavior of
\phi_{0}
can be computed explicitly
[
11
,
37
,
29
,
13
]
, revealing three characteristic regions with distinct asymptotic profiles:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,v)\eqsim\begin{cases}x^{\frac{1}{6}}\left(\frac{v^{3}}{x}\right)^{-\frac{5}{6}}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}\quad&\text{if }x<v^{3},\\
x^{\frac{1}{6}}\quad&\text{if }x\geq|v|^{3},\\
|v|^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad&\text{if }x<-v^{3}.\end{cases}

It is natural to suspect, (and, indeed, this is an adequate reformulation of the aforementioned question) that
all solutions
to the Kolmogorov equation

(1.8)

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\Delta_{v}f&=F\quad&&\text{in }\big((-1,1)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})=:\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}),\\
f&=g&&\text{on }\gamma_{-}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0}),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})
denotes a standard kinetic cylinder (see (
1.9
)), behave like the function
\phi_{0}
up to a smooth error term locally at any boundary point in
\gamma_{0}
, at least if
g=0
. Our main results confirm this conjecture, however they also demonstrate that the situation is significantly more complex in general domains and in the presence of source terms.

Our findings yield a precise description of the anisotropic nature of the singularity of solutions at
\gamma_{0}
. In order to state them, we need to decompose the domain near
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
into several different regions, similar to (
1.7
). We denote
d_{\Omega}(x)=\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
and write for
\epsilon\in(0,1]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\epsilon}:=\big\{d_{\Omega}^{\epsilon}(x)\leq(n_{x}\cdot v)^{3}\big\},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{+}=\{d_{\Omega}(x)\leq-(n_{x}\cdot v)^{3}\},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{0}=\{d_{\Omega}(x)\geq|n_{x}\cdot v|^{3}\}.

Here, if
x\in\Omega
, we denote by
n_{x}
the outer unit normal vector at the projection
\bar{x}\in\partial\Omega
of
x
to
\partial\Omega
, and without loss of generality, we assume that the projection is unique
4
4

4

We can always find a tubular neighborhood of
z_{0}
where the projection to the boundary is unique.
in
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})
.

Our third main result shows that solutions to the Kolmogorov equation maintain
higher regularity of order less than three up to the grazing set

\gamma_{0}
near the incoming boundary
\gamma_{-}
, i.e. as
v\cdot n_{x}\nearrow 0
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain and let
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Let
F\in C^{5/2}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))
and
g\in C^{2,1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0})\cap\gamma_{-})
. Let
f
be a solution to (
1.8
) in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0})
.

Then, it holds
f\in C^{3-\epsilon}(\mathcal{H}_{1/2}(z_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\epsilon})
, and

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{3-\epsilon}(\mathcal{H}_{1/2}(z_{0})\cap\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\epsilon})}\leq C\big(\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))}+[F]_{C^{5/2}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))}+[g]_{C^{2,1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0})\cap\gamma_{-})}\big),

where
C
depends only on
n,\epsilon,|z_{0}|,\Omega
.

This result remains true for solutions to kinetic Fokker-Planck equations (
1.2
) with smooth enough coefficients (see
Theorem 8.4
).

It is important to emphasize that, in contrast to
Theorem 1.3
(i), the norms in
Theorem 1.6
remain uniformly bounded up to the grazing set. This fact crucially sets
Theorem 1.6
apart from previous higher order regularity results in
\gamma_{-}

[
36
, Theorem 1.3]
,
[
35
, Proposition 4.5]
, and
[
40
, Theorem 1.2]
. Hence, to the best of our knowledge,
Theorem 1.6
is the first result confirming higher regularity up to
\gamma_{0}
in specific directions.

We point out that the regularity established in
Theorem 1.6
is sharp in the sense that solutions in general do not exceed
C^{3-\epsilon}
regularity. Indeed, we construct an explicit function which is globally
C^{1,1}
, and
C^{3-\epsilon}
in
\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\epsilon}
, and it solves (
1.1
) with
F\equiv 1
subject to absorbing boundaries (see
subsection 6.3
). Note that in the homogeneous case
F\equiv 0
, one could even show that solutions are
C^{\infty}
up to
\gamma_{0}
near
\gamma_{-}
by combining
[
36
, Theorem 1.3]
with interior estimates.

Regularity estimates up to
\gamma_{0}
above the critical regularity threshold
\frac{1}{2}
cannot hold in any other region than in
\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, which is the region considered in
Theorem 1.6
. In fact, such behavior is ruled out by the explicit solution
\phi_{0}
, which is merely
C^{1/2}
along the trajectory
x=v^{3}
, i.e. on the boundary of
\mathcal{R}_{-}^{1}
, and in
\mathcal{R}^{0}\cup\mathcal{R}^{+}
. However, as our next main result asserts, in these regions, all solutions (subject to absorbing boundary conditions) behave asymptotically like
\phi_{0}
up to an error term that is Lipschitz continuous.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain and let
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
. Let
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))
and
g\equiv 0
. Let
f
be a solution to (
1.8
) in
\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0})
and define
\Phi(t,x,v)=\phi_{0}(\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega),v\cdot n_{x})
.

Then, it holds

\displaystyle\frac{f}{\Phi}\in C^{0,1}\big(\mathcal{H}_{1/2}(z_{0})\setminus\mathcal{R}^{1}_{-}\big).

Moreover, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\|f/\Phi\|_{C^{0,1}(\mathcal{H}_{1/2}(z_{0})\setminus\mathcal{R}_{-}^{1})}\leq C\big(\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))}+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))}\big),

where
C
depends only on
n,|z_{0}|,\Omega
.

A variant of this theorem remains true for solutions to kinetic Fokker-Planck equations (
1.2
) with sufficiently smooth coefficients (see
Theorem 6.3
for a version in the half-space and
Theorem 8.6
for a result in a smooth convex domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
). In this case, the function
\Phi
has to be modified appropriately. Note that
\Phi^{2}
is always comparable to the kinetic distance to
\gamma_{0}
, denoted by
\mathrm{dist}_{\text{kin}}
(see (
8.16
)), up to higher order in the sense that

\displaystyle\Phi\asymp\mathrm{dist}_{\text{kin}}^{1/2},\qquad D^{\beta}\Phi\lesssim\mathrm{dist}_{\text{kin}}^{1/2-|\beta|}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0})\setminus\mathcal{R}^{1}_{-}.

Theorem 1.7
confirms that the function
\phi_{0}
precisely characterizes the boundary behavior of solutions to (
1.1
) near
\gamma_{0}
away from the incoming boundary. Note, however, that the asymptotic description of
Theorem 1.7
breaks down near the incoming boundary. Indeed, the function
\phi_{-1}
constructed in Appendix
C
is a solution to (
1.6
) and
\phi_{-1}/\phi_{0}
fails to be bounded on
\gamma_{-}
up to
\gamma_{0}
by (
C.38
).

Remark 1.8
.

In the setting of
Theorem 1.7
, the Lipschitz regularity is sharp in some sense, as we discuss in
subsection 6.2
. However, in certain special cases, the optimal regularity exponent is higher and regularity estimates can be achieved by following our technique, as we explain carefully in
subsection 6.2
:

•

In the half-space, one can show that the optimal regularity is
C^{3/2}
instead of
C^{0,1}
. This indicates that the presence of coefficients heavily complicates the boundary behavior of solutions.

•

In the half-space in case
F\equiv 0
, one can even prove that the optimal regularity is even
C^{1,1}
instead of
C^{0,1}
.

We emphasize that
Theorem 1.6
and
Theorem 1.7
seem to be the first results establishing regularity above the critical threshold of
\frac{1}{2}
for solutions to the Kolmogorov equation with absorbing boundaries. The work most closely related to ours is probably
[
17
]
, where the authors examine the precise boundary behavior of solutions to the Kolmogorov equation in the half-space in terms of
\phi_{0}
. However,
[
17
]
does not address the smoothness of the remainder terms. Conversely, their results include sharp time decay estimates of solutions.

1.3.
Strategy of the proof

The common key difficulty in the proofs of all our main results arises from the singular solution behavior at
\gamma_{0}
. The central novelty of this article is to characterize the higher order asymptotics of the solution near
\gamma_{0}
subject to the prescribed in-flow boundary condition. Note that this is more complicated for the in-flow condition than for specular reflection (see
[
35
]
), since solutions exhibit a much more singular behavior.

1.3.1.
Higher order expansions at
\gamma_{0}

The main idea to show
Theorem 1.3
,
Theorem 1.7
, and
Theorem 1.6
is to establish that all solutions to (
1.2
) behave like the explicit solution
\phi_{0}
at boundary points in
\gamma_{0}
up to error terms which possess higher smoothness.

Let us explain this result in the simplest case, i.e when
\Omega=\{x_{n}>0\}
is a half-space. We consider solutions in the intersection of
(-1,1)\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a kinetic cylinder

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{R}(z_{0})=\big\{(t,x,v)\in(t_{0}-R^{2},t_{0}+R^{2})\times B_{R^{3}}(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0})\times B_{R}(v_{0})\big\}.

Note that in the flat case, it holds
\gamma=(-1,1)\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}=(-1,1)\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}=0\},\quad\gamma_{\pm}=(-1,1)\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{\pm v_{n}<0\}.

Let us assume that
f
solves the Kolmogorov equation with prescribed in-flow

(1.10)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f-\Delta_{v}f&=F\quad&&\text{in }Q_{1}(z_{0})\cap\big((-1,1)\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)=:\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}),\\
f&=g&&\text{in }\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})\cap\big((-1,1)\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\}\big)\end{aligned}\right.

for some
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
, where
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{1}(z_{0}))
and
g\in C^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q}_{1}(z_{0})\cap\gamma_{-})
for some
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.

We establish the following expansion of order
\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
at the boundary point
z_{0}
for some
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
:

(1.11)

\displaystyle|f(z)-g(z_{0})-c_{0}\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})|\lesssim r^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall z=(t,x,v)\in\mathcal{H}_{r}(z_{0}).

Since the function
\phi_{0}
is
C^{1/2}
, by combination of (
1.11
) with interior estimates and boundary estimates away from the grazing set (see
[
35
]
), we manage to deduce that
f\in C^{1/2}
up to the grazing set. This proves our main result
Theorem 1.3
on the optimal regularity for the in-flow boundary condition.

We emphasize that (
1.11
) should be interpreted as a regularity estimate of order strictly greater than
\frac{1}{2}
, since it indicates that the function
z\mapsto f(z)-g(z_{0})-c_{0}\phi_{0}(z)
is
C^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}
locally at the point
z_{0}
.

Although it might be tempting to prove
Theorem 1.3
solely relying on barrier arguments based on the explicit solutions
\phi_{0}
, we could not reach the optimal
C^{1/2}
regularity via this approach due to the following reasons:

•

By perturbation arguments one would naturally lose
\epsilon
derivatives for a small
\epsilon>0
, when considering equations with coefficients (
1.2
).

•

It is unclear how to treat non-zero boundary data
g
. Since
g
is in general not
C^{2}
, one cannot simply “subtract” it from the solution to reduce the problem to a homogeneous one.

The expansion (
1.11
) allows to surpass all of these obstacles, while giving us higher order information on the solution behavior at the same time.

In order to prove our main results
Theorem 1.6
and
Theorem 1.7
on the precise solution behavior near
\gamma_{0}
, we require asymptotic expansions of even higher order
\beta<3+\frac{1}{2}
. In fact, our most general result in this direction is
section 5
, where we show that there exist polynomials

P\in\mathcal{P}_{\lfloor\beta\rfloor}
,
p_{\phi_{0}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\lfloor\beta-\frac{1}{2}\rfloor}
,
p_{\psi_{0}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\lfloor\beta-2\rfloor}
, and
p_{\phi_{1}}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
such that

(1.12)

\displaystyle|f(z)-P(z)-p_{\phi_{0}}(z)\phi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})-p_{\psi_{0}}(z)\psi_{0}(x_{n},v_{n})-p_{\phi_{1}}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1}(x_{n},v_{n})|\lesssim r^{\beta}\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall z\in\mathcal{H}_{r}(z_{0}).

This higher order expansion reveals a key feature of kinetic equations: the explicit 1D function
\phi_{0}
alone is
not sufficient to fully capture the boundary behavior
of solutions at the grazing set. Indeed, there are two more explicit solutions
\psi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
to the stationary Kolmogorov equation, which have to be included in the expansion as soon as
\beta>2
and
\beta>2+\frac{1}{2}
, respectively. The function
\phi_{1}
satisfies
\partial_{x}\phi_{1}=c_{1}\phi_{0}
for some
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and is yet another explicit solution of (
1.6
), which is homogeneous of degree
3+\frac{1}{2}
, and has very similar asymptotic behavior as the function
\phi_{0}
(see (
C.37
)). On the other hand, the function
\psi_{0}
solves

(1.13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}\psi_{0}-\partial_{vv}\psi_{0}&=1\quad&&\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},\\
\psi_{0}&=0&&\text{on }\{0\}\times(0,\infty),\end{aligned}\right.

and its boundary behavior is fundamentally different from the one of
\phi_{0}
. For instance, it turns out that
\psi_{0}
is homogeneous of degree two (instead of
\frac{1}{2}
), which implies that
\psi_{0}\in C^{1,1}
(instead of
C^{1/2}
) up to
\gamma_{0}
. On the other hand, the function
\psi_{0}
does not exhibit exponential decay at
\gamma_{-}
, but decays only polynomially. Precisely, its sharp behavior as
v\cdot n_{x}\nearrow 0
is such that
\psi_{0}\in C^{3-\epsilon}(\{x_{n}^{\epsilon}\leq v_{n}^{3}\})
for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
(see
subsection C.2
). Hence, due to (
1.12
), we are able to deduce that any solution to (
1.10
) exhibits the same regularity properties as
v\cdot n_{x}\nearrow 0
, which yields
Theorem 1.6
.

Remark 1.9
.

Note that (
1.12
) needs to be modified in case
\beta>3+\frac{1}{2}
. Indeed, one can find a countable family of solutions
\phi_{m}
to (
1.15
) that are homogeneous of degree
\frac{1}{2}+3m
and satisfy
\partial_{x}\phi_{m}=c_{m}\phi_{m-1}
for some constant
c_{m}\in\mathbb{R}
. Analogously, one can find higher order versions of the functions
\psi_{0}
. We compute these solutions explicitly in
Appendix C
and establish several properties that might be of independent interest. The higher the value of
\beta
, the more terms involving these functions (and their derivatives) would have to be included in the expansion. Although or approach would allow for it, we decided not to include expansions of order
\beta>3+\frac{1}{2}
in this paper.

1.3.2.
Liouville theorem

Already in the simplest case, i.e. when
f
solves (
1.10
), the proof of the expansions (
1.11
) and (
1.12
) is far from trivial.
We prove them by a blow-up argument at the boundary point
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
, which heavily relies on the following Liouville-type classification result of solutions in the half-space (see also
Theorem 4.1
):

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\beta\in(0,3+\frac{1}{2})
and
q
,
Q
be polynomials. Let
f
be a solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\Delta_{v}f&=Q\quad&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
f&=q\quad&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}>0\},\end{aligned}\right.

such that

(1.14)

\displaystyle|f(t,x,v)|\leq C(1+|t|^{1/2}+|x|^{1/3}+|v|)^{\beta}\qquad\forall(t,x,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}\geq 0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then, there exist polynomials
P
,
p_{\phi_{0}}
,
p_{\psi_{0}}
, and
p_{\phi_{1}}
such that
f
is of the form

\displaystyle f=P+p_{\phi_{0}}\phi_{0}+p_{\psi_{0}}\psi_{0}+p_{\phi_{1}}\partial_{v_{n}}\phi_{1}.

Note that a blow-up procedure based on a Liouville theorem to show boundary regularity for kinetic equations was recently developed in
[
35
]
for solutions with specular reflection condition. Yet, our proof of the Liouville theorem has to be fundamentally different from the one in
[
35
]
, making
Theorem 1.10
one of the main contributions of this article. In fact, the specular reflection condition resembles a Neumann-type boundary condition in the sense that for
Q=0
solutions in
\{x_{n}>0\}
can be extended to global solutions by mirror reflection, and hence they must be polynomials. Such a powerful tool is not available in our setting, so that we have to develop an entirely different approach, already for stationary solutions with
q=Q=0
in 1D, i.e.

(1.15)

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}f-\partial_{vv}f=0\quad\text{in }(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},\qquad f=0\quad\text{on }\{0\}\times(0,\infty).

In this case, all solutions satisfying (
1.14
) for
\beta<3+\frac{1}{2}
are given by
\phi_{0}
(up to a multiplicative constant). To prove it, we proceed as follows: First, by solving an ODE problem, we show that the only
homogeneous
solution (up to a multiplying constant) to (
1.15
) is given by
\phi_{0}
. From here, it remains to prove that all solutions to (
1.15
) are homogeneous, however this is far from obvious in our setting. While for elliptic equations or nonlocal problems, this can often be deduced from Sturm-Liouville type theorems (see
[
34
,
9
]
), such a tool is unavailable here since
\phi_{0}
does not belong to the corresponding weighted
L^{2}
space. Instead, our proof relies on the construction of explicit sub- and supersolutions which yield sharp upper and lower bounds for solutions to (
1.15
) near the boundary. These bounds imply a boundary Harnack-type principle for solutions to (
1.15
) and yield a 1D Liouville theorem of order
\beta<\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, which we can raise subsequently with the help of the maximum principle and a precise analysis of the asymptotic behavior of
\phi_{0}
.

1.3.3.
Equations with coefficients

In this paper we develop a unified approach which also allows us to establish expansions such as (
1.12
) for solutions to general linear kinetic Fokker-Planck equations with coefficients (
1.2
). Note that it is already necessary to consider non-translations invariant equations in order to prove results for the Kolmogorov equation (
1.1
) in general domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

In that case the expansion (
1.12
) and its proof have to undergo substantial modifications, some of which we comment on below:

•

At any point
z_{0}
, the function
\phi_{0}
(and also
\psi_{0}
) has to be rescaled in such a way that
v\partial_{x}\phi_{0}=A_{n,n}(z_{0})\partial_{vv}\phi_{0}
. Hence, the regularity of the coefficient matrix
A
is crucial in order to deduce regularity of solutions from expansions at boundary points
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
.

•

Due to the
z
-dependence of the coefficients and the non-diagonal structure of
A
, the (rescaled) function
\phi_{0}
will solve (
1.2
) with unbounded source terms
F
behaving like
\partial_{v}\phi_{0}
and
\partial_{vv}\phi_{0}
at the boundary. In order to compensate for this, we also have to incorporate functions of the following form in the expansion (
1.12
), which are not present in the translation invariant case,

\displaystyle v_{n}^{i}\partial_{v_{n}}^{(j)}\phi_{0},\quad v_{n}^{i}\partial_{v_{n}}^{(j)}\phi_{1},\quad v_{n}^{i}\partial_{v_{n}}^{(j)}\psi_{0},\qquad\text{ for }i,j\in\{0,1,2\}.

Since all of these functions create further error terms, it is a key difficulty in the proof to balance them out appropriately.

The aforementioned modifications in (
1.12
) arising due to the presence of coefficients lead to several non-trivial challenges in the proofs of the expansions and in our main results. For instance, we have to generalize the
C^{\alpha}
estimates from
[
36
,
39
]
to equations with source terms that might be in divergence-form or unbounded (see
subsection 5.1
). We discuss all of these complications in full detail in Section
5
. Our most general expansion result is given by
section 5
.

1.3.4.
Diffuse reflection

We close this subsection by making several comments on the proof of our main result on the diffuse reflection condition (see
Theorem 1.1
).

As we already mentioned, solutions satisfying the diffuse reflection condition have the same singular boundary behavior as solutions with prescribed in-flow due to
subsection 7.2
. Hence, in order to prove that solutions are globally
C^{1/2}
and
C^{\infty}
locally in
\gamma_{-}
, we need to show

(a)

\mathcal{N}f\in C^{1/2+\epsilon}
in
\gamma_{-}
up to
\gamma_{0}
for some
\epsilon>0
,

(b)

\mathcal{N}f\in C^{\infty}
locally in
\gamma_{-}
(away from
\gamma_{0}
),

so that
Theorem 1.1
follows by application of
Theorem 1.3
with
g=\mathcal{N}f
.

Verifying (a) and (b) is a nontrivial task, since the quantity depends on the values of the solution
f
in
\gamma_{+}
in an integrated way, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}f(t,x,v)=\mathcal{M}(t,x,v)h(t,x,v),\qquad\text{where}\qquad h(t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(t,x,w)(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}\textnormal{\,d}w.

In order to prove (a), we apply global regularity estimates for
f
of order
\alpha\in(0,1)
as they were established in
[
36
,
39
]
and combine them with regularity estimates away from
\gamma_{0}
(see
[
35
, Proposition 4.5]
), making use of the weight
(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}
, which vanishes at
\gamma_{0}
(see
section 7
(i)).

The proof of (b) is significantly more involved, since the function
f
is in general only
C^{1/2}
up to
\gamma_{0}
, and therefore, in principle, higher derivatives of order
\beta
explode as
D^{\beta}f\sim(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}^{-\beta+1/2}
as
(w\cdot n_{x})_{-}\to 0
.
We circumvent this issue by flattening the boundary, i.e. considering the problem for
\Omega=\{x_{n}>0\}
, and observing that in this case
n_{x}\equiv-e_{n}
becomes constant. Hence, the function
h
only depends on
(t,x^{\prime})
, which are tangential to the boundary. This means that we only need to prove higher regularity of
f
in
(t,x^{\prime})
on
\gamma_{+}
up to
\gamma_{0}
, in order to get higher regularity of
h
(and thus of
\mathcal{N}f
). We achieve this by taking increments
\delta_{h}f
of
f
in the tangential directions
(t,x^{\prime})
, and analyzing the linear kinetic Fokker-Planck equation satisfied by those increments (see (
7.20
) for their precise definition), which is (roughly) of the form

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f)\delta_{h}f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla\delta_{h}f)&=\delta_{h}F+\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)\quad&&\text{in }\{x_{n}>0\},\\
\delta_{h}f&=\delta_{h}(\mathcal{N}f)&&\text{on }\gamma_{-}\end{aligned}\right.

(see also (
7.29
)). Here, a key difficulty comes from the source term
\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)
, since the function
A\nabla_{v}f
is in general unbounded at
\gamma_{0}
since
f
is not better than
C^{1/2}
. We solve this issue by using the expansion (
1.12
) combined with higher order regularity results away from
\gamma_{0}
(see
subsection 7.1
and
subsection 7.1
) to deduce that
A\nabla_{v}f
belongs to a suitable
L^{p}
space so that regularity estimates for
\delta_{h}f
follow from
Theorem 1.3
by perturbation arguments. This procedure becomes increasingly more involved for higher order increments, and we have to develop a delicate inductive argument to deduce
C^{\infty}
regularity of
\mathcal{N}f
(see
section 7
(ii)).

1.4.
Outline

This article is structured as follows. In Section
2
we introduce the necessary function spaces and weak solutions concepts required for this work. Section
3
is dedicated to the proof of a Liouville theorem for stationary solutions in 1D with prescribed in-flow condition and in Section
4
, we generalize this result by proving a Liouville theorem in the half-space (see
Theorem 1.10
for a simplified version). Next, in Section
5
, we establish expansions at grazing boundary points
z_{0}\in\gamma_{0}
and prove
section 5
, which implies in particular (
1.11
), (
1.12
). In Sections
6
and
7
, we establish our main results for solutions to (
1.2
) with coefficients in the special case
\Omega=\{x_{n}>0\}
, and discuss their sharpness. In Section
8
, we extend these results to general domains, thereby establishing
Theorem 1.1
,
Theorem 1.3
,
Theorem 1.7
, and
Theorem 1.6
. Finally, this article comes with four appendices, the most notable one being Appendix
C
, where we find explicit countable families of homogeneous solutions to (
1.6
) with absorbing and prescribed in-flow conditions and derive fine estimates on their behavior close to
\gamma_{0}
. We believe these results to be of independent interest.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

Kyeongbae Kim and Marvin Weidner were supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG,
German Research Foundation) under Germany’s Excellence Strategy - EXC-2047/1 - 390685813 and through the CRC 1720.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce some notation, function spaces, introduce our notion of weak solutions, and provide several auxiliary lemmas.

In what follows, we denote general constants by
c
. When relevant, its dependence on parameters is specified in parentheses; for example,
c=c(n,\Lambda)
indicates that the constant
c
depends only on
n
and
\Lambda
. In addition, we write
a\eqsim b
, when
\frac{1}{c}a\leq b\leq cb
for some universal constant
c\geq 1
.

First, let us introduce some geometric notation:

•

We write points
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
as

\displaystyle X_{0}=(x_{0},v_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}.

In addition, we denote by
x_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
the first
n-1
components of
x_{0}=(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}
. In particular, for any
z_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0},v_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, we write
z_{0}^{\prime}=(t_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},v_{0}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2(n-1)+1}
, where
x_{0}=(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{0,n}),v_{0}=(v_{0}^{\prime},v_{0,n})
.

•

We recall the left group action defined by

\displaystyle z_{0}\circ z=(t_{0}+t,x_{0}+x+tv_{0},v+v_{0}),\quad z_{0}^{-1}=(-t_{0},-x_{0}+t_{0}v_{0},-v_{0}).

In particular, we write

|z_{0}-z_{1}|=|z_{1}^{-1}\circ z_{0}|\coloneqq\max\{|t_{0}-t_{1}|^{\frac{1}{2}},|x_{0}-(x_{1}+(t_{0}-t_{1})v_{1})|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{0}-v_{1}|\}

and

|X_{0}-X_{1}|=\max\{|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{0}-v_{1}|\}.

•

For any
r>0
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}
, we write
S_{r}z\coloneqq(r^{2}t,r^{3}x,rv)
.

•

For any
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write
\langle v_{0}\rangle\coloneqq 1+|v_{0}|
.

•

Kinetic cylinders and their intersection with a domain are defined as follows

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{R}(z_{0})=\left\{(t,x,v)\,:\,t\in(t_{0}-R^{2},t_{0}+R^{2})=:I_{R}(t_{0}),\penalty\ \penalty\ x\in B_{R^{3}}(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0}),\penalty\ \penalty\ v\in B_{R}(v_{0})\right\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{R}(z_{0})\coloneqq{\mathcal{Q}}_{R}(z_{0})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}).

•

Throughout the paper, we always consider a coefficient matrix
A(z):\mathbb{R}^{2n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
such that
A
is uniformly elliptic with ellipticity constant
\Lambda\geq 1
in the sense that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-1}I_{n}\leq A\leq\Lambda I_{n}.

•

Given a domain
(a,b)\times\Omega\times V\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\Omega
and
V
being bounded sets, we define the kinetic boundary of
(a,b)\times\Omega\times V
by

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}((a,b)\times\Omega\times V)=(\{t=a\}\times\Omega\times V)\cup((a,b)\times\Omega\times\{v\in\partial V\})\cup\gamma_{-}.

We recall the kinetic distance and kinetic Hölder spaces as in
[
27
]
. For any
z_{1},z_{2}
, we write

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(z_{1},z_{2})\coloneqq\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\{\max\left\{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\frac{1}{2}},|x_{1}-(x_{2}+(t_{1}-t_{2})w|^{\frac{1}{3}},|v_{1}-w|,|v_{2}-w|\right\}\right\}.

Then we observe
|z_{1}-z_{2}|\eqsim\mathrm{dist}(z_{1},z_{2})
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, we write

\displaystyle C^{k,\epsilon}(D)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{f:D\to\mathbb{R}\,:\,\|f\|_{C_{\ell}^{k,\epsilon}(D)}\coloneqq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(D)}+[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(D)}<\infty\},

\displaystyle[f]_{C^{k,\epsilon}(D)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{z_{1}\in D}\sup_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\sup_{z_{1}\neq z_{2}\in D}\frac{|(f-p)(z_{2})|}{\mathrm{dist}(z_{1},z_{2})^{k+\epsilon}},

and we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
the space of kinetic polynomials of kinetic degree
k
(see
[
35
]
for the precise definition).
Note that we use the following convention. When we specify
f\in C^{\alpha}(\mathcal{H}_{R}(z_{0}))
with
\alpha\leq 0
, it means
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{R}(z_{0}))
. In the same way,
[f]_{C^{\alpha}(\mathcal{H}_{R}(z_{0}))}=\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}_{R}(z_{0}))}
when
\alpha\leq 0
.

Moreover, we will use the notation
C^{k,\alpha}=C^{k+\alpha}
interchangeably whenever
k+\alpha\not\in\mathbb{N}
.

In addition, we denote by a
k
-the order differential operator as
{D}^{k}
. For example
{D}^{2}
can be
\partial_{v_{i},v_{j}}
or
\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}
, and
D^{3}
can be
\partial_{v_{i},v_{j},v_{k}}
,
\partial_{v_{i}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})
or
\partial_{x_{i}}
.

2.1.
Weak solution concept

We now describe the definition of a weak solution to kinetic Fokker-Planck equations at the boundary.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. Let
B\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q})
,
F\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Q})
and
g\in L^{\infty}(\gamma_{-})
. We say that
f
is a weak solution to

(2.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)&=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{Q}$},\\
f&=g&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{Q}$},\end{aligned}\right.

if
\nabla_{v}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Q})
and
f
satisfies

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{Q}}f(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})\psi\,dz+\int_{\mathcal{Q}}A\nabla_{v}f\nabla_{v}\psi\,dz=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}B\cdot\nabla_{v}f\psi\,dz-\int_{\gamma_{-}}g\psi(v\cdot n)\,d\gamma+\int_{\mathcal{Q}}F\psi\,dz

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1+2n})
with
\supp\psi\cap\partial Q\subset\gamma_{-}\cup\gamma_{0}
.

When
\mathcal{Q}=\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
h
is a weak solution to (
2.1
) if
h\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\{x_{n}>0\};H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, and

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{Q}}f(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})\psi\,dz+\int_{\mathcal{Q}}A\nabla_{v}f\nabla_{v}\psi\,dz=\int_{\mathcal{Q}}B\cdot\nabla_{v}f\psi\,dz-\int_{\gamma_{-}}g\psi(-v_{n})\,d\gamma+\int_{\mathcal{Q}}F\psi\,dz

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1+2n})
with
\supp\psi\cap\partial\mathcal{Q}\subset\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}\geq 0\}
.

Note that the definition implies that
v\cdot\nabla_{x}f\in L^{2}_{t,x}H_{v}^{-1}(\mathcal{Q})
by the same argument as in
[
36
, p.15-16]
.

Remark 2.2
.

Note that if the function
f\in C(\overline{\mathcal{Q}})
with
\nabla_{v}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Q})
,
v\cdot\nabla_{x}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{Q})
and
f=g
on
\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}\mathcal{Q}
satisfies in the classical sense

(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\Delta_{v}f=F\quad\text{in }\mathcal{Q},

then it is a weak solution to (
2.1
). This was already observed in [Definition 5.1 Sil 22].

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f\in C(\overline{\mathcal{H}_{R}})
be a classical solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} (\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\Delta_{v}f&=F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathcal{H}_{R}$},\\
f&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\gamma_{-}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{R}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
F,G\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
. If
\nabla_{v}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
, then
f
is a weak solution.

Proof.

Let us take a function
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1+2n})
with
\supp\psi\cap\partial\mathcal{H}_{R}\subset\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}\geq 0\}
. Since
f
is a classical solution and
F\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}F\psi+G\nabla_{v}\psi\,dz=\int_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}-\Delta_{v})f\psi\,dz\eqqcolon J.

Now using the integration by parts together with the fact that
f\in C(\overline{H_{R}})
,
\nabla_{v}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{R})
,
\supp\psi\cap\partial\mathcal{H}_{R}\subset\{x_{n}=0\}\times\{v_{n}\geq 0\}
and
f\equiv 0
in
\gamma_{-}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{R}
, we estimate
J
as

\displaystyle J=\int_{\mathcal{H}_{R}}-f(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})\psi+\nabla_{v}f\nabla_{v}\psi\,dz.

Now plugging this into (
2.2
) shows that
f
is a weak solution. This completes the proof.
∎

2.2.
Interior regularity

Here, we present two interior regularity results for kinetic Fokker-Planck equations.
First, we recall the interior regularity estimates from
[
24
, Corollary 3.3]
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
g
be a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})g-\Delta_{v}g=0\quad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}_{R}.

Then for any nonnegative integer
k
,

\displaystyle\|D^{k}g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{R/2})}\leq cR^{-k}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{R})}

for some constant
c=c(n,k)
.

Next, we provide interior Schauder type estimates in divergence form.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f
be a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f-\mathrm{div}_{v}(A\nabla_{v}f)=B\cdot\nabla_{v}f+F-\mathrm{div}_{v}(G)\quad\text{in }\mathcal{Q}_{1}.

If
A\in C^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
,
B\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
,
F\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
, and
G\in C^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})
, then we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{1,\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q}_{\frac{1}{2}})}\leq c(\|f\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})}+[G]_{C^{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})),

where
c=c(n,\Lambda,\epsilon,\|A\|_{C^{\epsilon}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})},\|B\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Q}_{1})})
.

Since the proof closely follows the argument in
[
30
]
, we defer the details to
Appendix B
.

3.
A Liouville theorem for stationary solutions in 1D

The goal of this section is to prove a Liouville theorem in the half-space in 1D (see
Theorem 3.15
). Its proof is split into several steps.

•

First, in Subsection
3.2
we establish an expansion of order
\frac{1}{2}+\alpha
for some small
\alpha>0
at
(0,0)
in terms of
\phi_{0}
. In particular, it yields Hölder continuity of
h/\phi
in
\mathcal{R}_{0}
and already implies a Liouville theorem for functions that do not grow faster than
1+|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\alpha}
.

•

Second, in Subsection
3.3
we improve the order of the Liouville theorem by a delicate argument based on the maximum principle and the asymptotic behavior of
\phi_{0}
to functions that do not grow faster than
1+|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+3-\epsilon}
.

•

Third, in Subsection
3.4
we find explicit homogeneous solutions to inhomogeneous equations in the half-space with in-flow condition. A combination with the Liouville theorem from the previous step concludes the proof of
Theorem 3.15
.

We start by introducing stationary kinetic cylinders in dimension one, as follows:

(3.1)

\displaystyle Q_{R}(x_{0},v_{0})=(x_{0}-R^{3},x_{0}+R^{3})\times(v_{0}-R,v_{0}+R),

\displaystyle H_{R}(x_{0},v_{0})=(x_{0}-R^{3},x_{0}+R^{3})\times(v_{0}-R,v_{0}+R)\cap\{x>0\}\times\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle H_{R}^{+}\coloneqq((R/2)^{3},R^{3})\times(-R,R).

3.1.
Harnack’s type inequality for the stationary case

First, let us introduce a suitable notion of weak solutions to the stationary version of the kinetic Fokker-Planck equation in align with (
2.1
)

Definition 3.1
.

Let
Q=(a,b)\times(c,d)
and
g\in C(\overline{Q})
. We say that
h
is a weak solution to

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h&=F&&\qquad\mbox{in $Q$},\\
h&=g&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}Q$},\end{aligned}\right.

if
h\in L^{2}((a,b);H^{1}(c,d))
and
h
satisfies

\displaystyle-\iint_{Q}h(v\partial_{x}\psi)\,dx\,dv+\iint_{Q}\partial_{v}h\partial_{v}\psi\,dx\,dv=-\int_{\gamma_{-}}g\psi(v\cdot n)\,d\gamma+\int_{Q}F\psi\,dx\,dv

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\supp\psi\cap\partial Q\subset\gamma_{-}\cup\gamma_{0}
.

When
Q=(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, we say that
h
is a weak solution to (
3.2
) if
h\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(0,\infty;H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}))
, and

(3.3)

\displaystyle-\iint_{Q}h(v\partial_{x}\psi)\,dx\,dv+\iint_{Q}\partial_{v}h\partial_{v}\psi\,dx\,dv=-\int_{\{x=0\}\times\{v>0\}}g\psi(-v)\,d\gamma

for any
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\supp\psi\cap\partial Q\subset\{x=0\}\times\{v\geq 0\}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Now, we give two remarks about the existence and regularity of solutions to (
3.2
).

•

Note that when
Q
is a bounded domain and
g\in C^{\infty}(\overline{Q})
, by
[
3
, Theorem 1, Theorem 3 and Definition 3.3]
, there is a unique weak solution
h
to (
3.2
). Note that, by the remark given after
[
36
, Definition 5.1]
and by
[
3
, Lemma 3.1, Definition 3.3]
, the notion of a weak solution given in
[
3
]
coincides with the one in
subsection 3.1
.

•

By considering
h(t,x,v)=h(x,v)
, we observe that
h
is also a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})h-\partial_{vv}h=F\quad\text{in }(-1,0]\times Q

with
h=g
on
I\times\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}Q
. Thus, by
[
36
]
and
subsection 2.2
, if
g\in C^{\alpha}(\overline{Q})
and
F\in C^{\infty}(Q)
, then we have
h\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}(Q)\cap C^{\alpha}(\overline{Q})
.

In this subsection, we denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
the one-sided kinetic cylinder defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}(z_{0})\coloneqq\left\{z\,:\,t\in(t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}],\,v\in B_{r}(v_{0}),\,x\in B_{r^{3}}(x_{0}+(t-t_{0})v_{0})\right\}.

First, based on the parabolic version of Harnack’s inequality given in
[
12
]
, we provide Harnack’s inequality for the stationary case. The cylinders are slanted in
x
due to the underlying kinetic geometry.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
h\geq 0
be a weak solution to

(3.4)

v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h=0\quad\text{in }Q.

There are constants
\theta,\gamma\in(0,1/2)
, and
c\geq 1
such that for any
R\in(0,2]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{A_{\gamma}\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h\leq c\inf_{A_{0}\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h,

where

\displaystyle A_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{x\,:\,|x-x_{0}-v_{0}(t-R^{2}\gamma)|<(\theta R)^{3},\,t\in(-(R\theta)^{2},0]\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\big(x_{0}-v_{0}R^{2}(\gamma+\theta^{2})-(\theta R)^{3},x_{0}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma+(\theta R)^{3}\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ if }v_{0}>0,\\
\big(x_{0}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma-(\theta R)^{3},x_{0}-v_{0}R^{2}(\theta^{2}+\gamma)+(\theta R)^{3}\big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ if }v_{0}<0\end{cases},

whenever
(x_{0},v_{0})
is such that
\{x\,:\,|x-(x_{0}+v_{0}t)|<R^{3}\,:t\in(-R^{2},0]\}\times B_{R}(v_{0})\subset Q
.

Proof.

Note that if
\{x\,:\,|x-(x_{0}+v_{0}t)|<R^{3}\,:t\in(-R^{2},0]\}\times B_{R}(v_{0})\subset Q
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{R}(0,x_{0},v_{0})\subset(-R^{2},0]\times Q
.
Next, we observe that
h=h(t,x,v)
is a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\partial_{x})h-\partial_{vv}h=0\quad\text{in }(-R^{2},0]\times Q.

Now, applying the interior Harnack inequality given in
[
12
]
that there are constants
\theta,\gamma\in(0,1/2)
, and
c\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\theta R}\left(-R^{2}\gamma,x_{0},v_{0}\right)}h\leq c\inf_{\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\theta R}(0,x_{0},v_{0})}h

for some constant
c\geq 1
. Therefore, we deduce the desired estimate.
∎

As usual, the Harnack inequality can be applied iteratively (Harnack chains) to compare the values of the solution also in more general domains. In particular, we can show a comparability result for the values of
h(\cdot,v_{0})
for any fixed
v_{0}
. Due to the underlying kinetic geometry, the ”direction“ of the comparability depends heavily on the sign of
v_{0}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
h\geq 0
be a weak solution to

v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h=0\quad\mbox{in $H_{\mathcal{R}}$}.

If
v_{0}\in[0,\mathcal{R}/2]
, then for any
0<x_{0}\leq x_{1}<\mathcal{R}^{3}/2
, we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle h(x_{0},v_{0})\leq ch(x_{1},v_{0}),

where the constant
c
depends only on
x_{0}
,
v_{0}
, and
\mathcal{R}
.

If
v_{0}\in[-\mathcal{R}/2,0)
, then for any
0\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}<\mathcal{R}^{3}/2
, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle h(x_{1},v_{0})\leq ch(x_{0},v_{0}),

where the constant
c
depends only on
|v_{0}|
and
\mathcal{R}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Note that it is possible to choose
x_{0}=0
in case
v_{0}<0
. In addition, the constant
c
of (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) also depends on
\theta,\gamma\in(0,1/2)
which are determined in
subsection 3.1
. However, both constants
\theta,\gamma
are universal so that we just write the constant
c
depends only on
x_{0},v_{0},\mathcal{R}
or
|v_{0}|,\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

We prove the statements for
v_{0}>0
and
v_{0}<0
separately.

•

Let
v_{0}\geq 0
and
0<x_{0}<x_{1}
. First, note that there is
R_{0}\leq\mathcal{R}/2
such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle x_{0}-v_{0}(2R_{0})^{2}-(2R_{0})^{3}=0,

as
g(r)=x_{0}-v_{0}(2r)^{2}-(2r)^{3}
is non-increasing when
r\geq 0
and

(3.8)

\displaystyle g\left(\left(\frac{x_{0}}{16v_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)>0>g\left(\left(\frac{x_{0}}{4v_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

Moreover, (
3.8
) yields

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{x_{0}}{16v_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}<R_{0}<\left(\frac{x_{0}}{4v_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which will ensure that (
3.5
) only depends on
R_{0}
through
x_{0}
and
v_{0}
. We now choose

R_{1}\coloneqq{\min\left\{R_{0},\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}-x_{0}}}{2(1+\sqrt{v_{0}})}\right\}}

to see that there is a nonnegative integer
k
such that

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{k+1}\leq v_{0}R_{1}^{2}\gamma<\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{k},

where the constant
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
is determined in
subsection 3.1
. Note that by the definition of
R_{1}
and due to (
3.9
), there is some
k_{0}>0
, depending only on
x_{0},v_{0}
, and
\mathcal{R}
, such that
k\leq k_{0}
. Thus, there is
R\leq R_{1}
such that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{{k_{0}+1}}=v_{0}R^{2}\gamma.

We now define

(3.11)

\displaystyle y_{j}\coloneqq x_{1}-(\theta R)^{3}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma j

to see that

(3.12)

\mbox{$x_{1}=y_{0}+(\theta R)^{3}$ and $x_{0}=y_{k_{0}+1}+(\theta R)^{3}$}.

In addition, we observe from (
3.7
) and (
3.10
) that

\displaystyle\{x\,:\,|x-(y_{j}+v_{0}t)|<R^{3}\,:\,t\in(-R^{2},0]\}\subset(0,\mathcal{R}^{3})

for any
j=0,1,\ldots,k_{0}+1
.
Now using (
3.11
) and applying
subsection 3.1
with
x_{0}
replaced by
y_{j}
, we deduce

\displaystyle\sup_{(y_{j+1}-v_{0}\theta^{2}R^{2}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j+1}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h

\displaystyle=\sup_{(y_{j}-v_{0}(\theta^{2}+\gamma)R^{2}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h

\displaystyle\leq c\inf_{(y_{j}-v_{0}\theta^{2}R^{2}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h

Now using a Harnack chain together with (
3.12
), we derive

(3.13)

\displaystyle h(x_{0},v_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(y_{k+1}-v_{0}\theta^{2}R^{2}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{k+1}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h

\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq c^{k+1}\inf_{(y_{0}-v_{0}\theta^{2}R^{2}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{0}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}h\leq c^{k_{0}+1}h(x_{1},v_{0}).

•

Let
v_{0}<0
and
0\leq x_{0}\leq x_{1}
. Note that
h=h(t,x,v)
is a weak solution to

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+v\partial_{x})h-\partial_{vv}h=0\quad\text{in }(-\mathcal{R}^{2},0]\times H_{\mathcal{R}}.

Since
\{x=0\}\times[-2|v_{0}|,-|v_{0}|/4]\subset\gamma_{+}
, by
[
36
]
and
[
35
]
,
h
is smooth in
[0,7\mathcal{R}^{3}/8]\times[-2|v_{0}|,-|v_{0}|/4]
. Thus, there is a smooth extension
H\geq 0
of
h
that defined in
(-3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4)\times(-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2)
such that
H=h
in
[0,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4]\times[-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2]
. By
subsection 3.1
, there is a unique weak solution
F\geq 0
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}F-\partial_{vv}F&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(-3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4)\times(-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2)$},\\
F&=H&&\qquad\mbox{on $\{x=3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4\}\times[-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2]\cup[-3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4]\times\{v=-|v_{0}|\pm|v_{0}|/2\}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
h
is a smooth solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4)\times(-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2)$},\\
h&=H&&\qquad\mbox{on $\{x=3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4\}\times[-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2]\cup[0,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4]\times\{v=-|v_{0}|\pm|v_{0}|/2\}$},\end{aligned}\right.

by
subsection 3.1
,
F=h
in
[0,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4]\times[-3|v_{0}|/2,-|v_{0}|/2]
. As
|v_{0}|\leq\mathcal{R}/2
and
x_{1}\leq\mathcal{R}^{3}/2
, the constant

R_{0}\coloneq\frac{\mathcal{R}}{4(\sqrt{|v_{0}|}+1)}

satisfies

x_{1}-v_{0}(2R_{0})^{2}+(2R_{0})^{3}\leq 3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4.

We now proceed in a similar way as in the first step to find a suitable sequence as in (
3.11
).

First, we select

R_{1}\coloneqq\min\left\{R_{0},\frac{\sqrt{x_{1}-x_{0}}}{2(1+\sqrt{|v_{0}|})}\right\}

to see that there is a nonnegative integer
k
such that

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{{k+1}}\leq-v_{0}R_{1}^{2}\gamma<\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{k}.

As before, there is
k_{0}>0
, depending only on
v_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}
such that
k\leq k_{0}
.
Moreover, there is
R
such that

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}-x_{0}}{{k_{0}+1}}=-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma.

By taking
y_{j}\coloneqq x_{0}+(\theta R)^{3}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma j
, we get

(3.14)

\displaystyle x_{0}=y_{0}-(\theta R)^{3}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{1}=y_{k_{0}+1}-(\theta R)^{3}

and

\displaystyle\{x\,:\,|x-(y_{j}+v_{0}t)|<R^{3}\,:\,t\in(-R^{2},0]\}\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})\subset Q

for any
j=0,1,\ldots,k_{0}
, where we write
Q=(-3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4,3\mathcal{R}^{3}/4)\times(-3v_{0}/2,-v_{0}/2)
.

We are now going to apply
subsection 3.1
with
x_{0}
replaced by
y_{j}
to the function
F
to see that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\sup_{(y_{j+1}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j+1}-v_{0}R^{2}\theta^{2}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}F

\displaystyle=\sup_{(y_{j}-v_{0}R^{2}\gamma-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j}-v_{0}R^{2}(\theta^{2}+\gamma)+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}F

\displaystyle\leq c\inf_{(y_{j}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{j}-v_{0}R^{2}\theta^{2}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}F,

where we have used (
3.14
) for the first equality.

As in (
3.13
), along with (
3.14
) and (
3.15
), we deduce

(3.16)

\displaystyle h(x_{1},v_{0})=F(x_{1},v_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{(y_{k_{0}+1}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{k_{0}+1}-v_{0}R^{2}\theta^{2}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}F

\displaystyle\leq\cdots\leq c^{k_{0}+1}\inf_{(y_{0}-(\theta R)^{3},y_{0}-v_{0}R^{2}\theta^{2}+(\theta R)^{3})\times B_{\theta R}(v_{0})}F

\displaystyle\leq c^{k_{0}+1}F(x_{0},v_{0})=c^{k_{0}+1}h(x_{0},v_{0}).

By (
3.13
) and (
3.16
), we get the desired result.
∎

3.2.
An expansion in 1D

Throughout this subsection, we denote
\phi:=\phi_{0}
, where
\phi_{0}
is defined as

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,v)=\begin{cases}x^{\frac{1}{6}}U\left(-\frac{1}{6},\frac{2}{3},-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v<0,\\
\frac{1}{6}x^{\frac{1}{6}}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}U\left(\frac{5}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)&\quad\text{if }v\geq 0.\end{cases}

See also (
C.11
). The goal of this subsection is to prove the following oscillation estimate for
h/\phi
at
(0,0)
, where
h
is a solution to the stationary Kolmogorov’s equation with absorbing boundary condition in 1D:

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
R\geq 1
and
h
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $H_{R}$},\\
h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\{x=0\}\times(0,R)$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then we have for some constants
c\geq 1
and
R_{0}\geq 1
,

(3.17)

\displaystyle|h/\phi|\leq cR^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}\quad\text{in }H_{R/R_{0}}

and

(3.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{H_{rR}}\frac{h}{\phi}-\inf_{H_{rR}}\frac{h}{\phi}\leq cr^{\alpha}R^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{R})}\quad\text{for any }r\leq 1/R_{0}.

The proof of
subsection 3.2
goes in several steps.

First, we prove the following lemma which yields a pointwise lower bound for any nonnegative solution
h
in the half-space in terms of
\phi
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
h\geq 0
be a weak solution to

(3.19)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,4^{3})\times(-4,4)$},\\
h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{on $\{x=0\}\times(0,4)$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle\inf_{H^{+}_{2}}\frac{h}{\phi}\leq c\inf_{H_{1}}\frac{h}{\phi}.

Proof.

Let us denote

\displaystyle m_{0}\coloneqq\inf_{H_{2}^{+}}\frac{h}{\phi}

and assume
m_{0}>0
, otherwise the proof is done. Then we have
h\geq m_{0}\phi
in
H_{2}^{+}
, which implies

(3.21)

\displaystyle h\geq\frac{m_{0}}{c_{0}}\quad\text{in }H_{2}^{+}

for some constant
c_{0}\geq 1
, where we have also used that
\phi\geq c_{0}^{-1}
in
H_{2}^{+}
by (
C.36
).

We now divide the remaining proof into three steps.

Step 1.
First, we are going to construct a suitable barrier. To do so, let us define a function
{\psi}
by

\displaystyle{\psi}(x,v)\coloneqq(v-2)^{2},

and observe that

(3.22)

\displaystyle{\psi(v)}\in[1,16]\quad\text{on }[-2,1],\quad{\psi}^{\prime}(v)\leq 0\quad\text{on }(-\infty,2],\quad{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(v)\geq 0,\quad\text{and}\quad{\psi}(2)=0.

We now want to prove that the function
\varphi\coloneqq\phi\psi
is a subsolution to

(3.23)

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}\varphi-\partial_{vv}\varphi=0\quad\text{in }(0,2^{3}]\times(-2,2).

To this end, we use the terms
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
given in (
C.23
), and (
C.24
) with
\lambda_{m}=\frac{1}{6}
,
b=\frac{2}{3}
and
a_{m}=\frac{5}{6}
, along with (
C.1
) and (
C.9
) to see that

\displaystyle\partial_{v}\phi(x,v)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{6}x^{\frac{1}{6}}\left(-\frac{v^{2}}{3x}\right)U\left(-\frac{1}{6}+1,\frac{2}{3}+1,-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)<0&\quad\text{if }v<0,\\
\frac{1}{6}x^{\frac{1}{6}}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}\left(-\frac{v^{2}}{3x}\right)\left[U\left(\frac{5}{6},\frac{2}{3},\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)+\frac{5}{6}U\left(\frac{5}{6}+1,\frac{2}{3}+1,\frac{v^{3}}{9x}\right)\right]<0&\quad\text{if }v\geq 0.\end{cases}

In light of the fact that
\partial_{v}\phi\leq 0
and
\phi\geq 0
, which follows from (
C.36
), we derive

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}\varphi-\partial_{vv}\varphi=(v\partial_{x}\phi-\partial_{vv}\phi)\psi-2\partial_{v}\psi\partial_{v}\phi-\partial_{vv}\psi\phi=-2\partial_{v}\psi\partial_{v}\phi-\partial_{vv}\psi\phi\leq 0,

when
v\leq 2
. This implies (
3.23
).

Step 2.
We next prove that for any
x\in[0,1]
,

(3.24)

\displaystyle{h(1,-2)}\leq ch(x,-2).

This follows from (
3.5
) in
subsection 3.1
with
x_{1}=1
,
v_{0}=-2
, and
x_{0}=x\in[0,1]
. In addition, by following the proof of (
3.5
), we deduce that the constant
c
is universal.

Step 3.
We now use the supersolution
\varphi
given in (
3.23
) and the estimate (
3.24
) to derive the desired result with the help of the maximum principle. By (
3.21
) and (
3.24
) we get

\displaystyle h\geq\frac{m_{0}}{c_{1}}\quad\text{on }\left([0,1]\times\{v=-2\}\right)\cup\left(\{x=1\}\cup[-2,0]\right)\eqqcolon\Gamma_{1}

for some constant
c_{1}\geq 1
. By (
C.36
) there is a constant
c_{2}\geq 1
such that
\frac{\phi}{c_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{16c_{1}}
on
\Gamma_{1}
. Therefore, we derive

\displaystyle\frac{m_{0}\varphi}{c_{2}}=\frac{m_{0}\phi\psi}{c_{2}}\leq\frac{16m_{0}\phi}{c_{2}}\leq\frac{m_{0}}{c_{1}}\leq h\quad\text{on }\Gamma_{1},

where we have also used the first observation in (
3.22
). In addition, since
\psi(2)=0
and
\phi=0
on
\{x=0\}\times(0,2]
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{m_{0}\varphi}{c_{2}}=\frac{m_{0}\phi\psi}{c_{2}}=0\leq h\quad\text{on }([0,1]\times\{v=2\})\cup(\{x=0\}\times[0,2])=\Gamma_{2}.

Using this and (
3.23
), we have

\left\{\begin{aligned} (v\partial_{x}-\partial_{vv})(m_{0}\varphi/c_{2}-h)&\leq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,1)\times(-2,2)$},\\
m_{0}\varphi/c_{2}&\leq h&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}((0,1)\times(-2,2))=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}$},\end{aligned}\right.

which implies
m_{0}\varphi/c_{2}\leq h
in
H_{1}
by the maximum principle (see
[
3
, Theorem 3]
). Using the fact that
\psi(v)\in[1,9]
for
v\in[-1,1]
by (
3.22
), the desired estimate follows. This completes the proof.
∎

We next provide a variant of the interior Harnack estimate.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
h\geq 0
be a weak solution to (
3.19
). Then we have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\sup_{H_{2\theta}^{+}}\frac{h}{\phi}\leq c\inf_{H_{2\theta}^{+}}\frac{h}{\phi},

where the constant
\theta\in(0,1)
is determined in
subsection 3.1
.

Proof.

By
subsection 3.1
with
v_{0}=0
,
R=2
, and
x_{0}=9\theta^{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{(\theta^{3},8\theta^{3})\times B_{2\theta}}h\leq\sup_{\{|x-9\theta^{3}|<(2\theta)^{3}\}\times B_{2\theta}}h\leq c\inf_{\{|x-9\theta^{3}|<(2\theta)^{3}\}\times B_{2\theta}}h\leq c\inf_{(\theta^{3},8\theta^{3})\times B_{2\theta}}h,

as
\{|x-9\theta^{3}|<(2\theta)^{3}\}\times B_{2\theta}\subset H_{4}
. Since
\theta
is a universal constant, we get
\frac{1}{c}\leq\phi\leq c
in
H_{2\theta}^{+}
by (
C.36
) for some constant
c\geq 1
. Therefore, the desired estimate follows immediately.
∎

Our next goal is to prove a suitable upper bound for solutions
h
to (
3.19
) in terms of
\phi
(see
subsection 3.2
). As a first step, we show that
h
vanishes to infinite order at
\gamma_{-}
. The proof follows the same arguments as the ones given in
[
40
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{R}>0
and
h
be a weak solution to

(3.26)

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}h-\partial_{vv}h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $(0,(2\mathcal{R})^{3})\times(-2\mathcal{R},2\mathcal{R})$},\\
h&=0&&\qquad\mbox{on $\{x=0\}\times(0,(2\mathcal{R})^{3})$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Fix
v_{0},R>0
such that
v_{0}\geq R
. Then there is a constant
C_{1}\geq 1
such that for any
x\in[0,R^{3}/2^{10}]
,

(3.27)

\displaystyle|h(x,v_{0})|\leq 2e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{x}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)},

whenever
Q\coloneqq[0,x]\times[v_{0}-\sqrt{2}R/8,v_{0}+\sqrt{2}R/8]\subset(0,(2\mathcal{R})^{3})\times(-2\mathcal{R},2\mathcal{R})
.

Proof.

Let us fix
r\leq R^{3}/C_{0}
, where the constant
C_{0}
will be determined later (see (
3.37
)). Define a quasi-distance function by

\displaystyle\rho(x,v)\coloneqq\left(\frac{R^{2}}{4r}(x+r)^{2}-R(x+r)(v-v_{0})+64r(v-v_{0})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Then we observe

\displaystyle\rho(0,v_{0})=\frac{Rr^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho(r,v_{0})=Rr^{\frac{1}{2}},

and

(3.28)

\displaystyle[0,r]\times\left\{v=v_{0}\pm\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R\right\}\subset\left\{(x,v)\,:\,2R^{2}r\leq\rho^{2}(x,v)\leq 4R^{2}r\quad\text{and}\quad x\geq 0\right\}.

We are now going to prove that

(3.29)

\displaystyle h(x,v)\leq\left(\frac{v_{0}}{4c_{2}r^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}r}\right)\rho^{2}(x,v)e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{c_{2}\rho^{2}}+\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{2c_{2}r}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\quad\text{in }Q_{r}

for some constant
c_{2}\geq 1
, where

(3.30)

Q_{r}\coloneqq[0,r]\times\left[v_{0}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R,v_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R\right].

To do this, we divide the proof into three steps.

Step 1.
Construct a suitable increasing convex function.
To do this, we fix a constant
c_{2}\geq 1
which will be determined later (see at the end of
Step 2
). Let us define for
\xi\geq 0
,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\Phi(\xi)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\xi}e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{c_{2}\tau}}\,d\tau

to see that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{c_{2}}\Phi^{\prime}(\tau)=0.

Since

\displaystyle(\tau^{2}\Phi^{\prime}(\tau))^{\prime}=\tau^{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+2\tau\Phi^{\prime}(\tau)=(c_{2}^{-1}v_{0}R^{4}+2\tau)\Phi^{\prime}(\tau),

we get

(3.33)

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\Phi^{\prime}(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\tau}(c_{2}^{-1}v_{0}R^{4}+2s)\Phi^{\prime}(s)\,ds\leq(c_{2}^{-1}v_{0}R^{4}+2\tau)\Phi(\tau),

where we have used the fundamental theorem of calculus together with the fact that
\Phi^{\prime}(s)\geq 0
and
\Phi(0)=0
. Thus, by setting

(3.34)

\displaystyle\Psi(\xi)\coloneqq\frac{\Phi(\xi)}{\Phi(2R^{2}r)},

we derive

(3.35)

\displaystyle\Psi(\xi)=\frac{\Phi(\xi)-\Phi(0)}{\Phi(2R^{2}r)}\leq\frac{c_{2}^{-1}v_{0}R^{4}+4R^{2}r}{(2R^{2}r)^{2}}\frac{\xi\Phi^{\prime}(\xi)}{\Phi^{\prime}(2R^{2}r)}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{2}^{-1}v_{0}R^{4}+4R^{2}r}{(2R^{2}r)^{2}}\frac{\xi e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{c_{2}\xi}}}{\Phi^{\prime}(2R^{2}r)}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{v_{0}}{4c_{2}r^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}r}\right)\xi e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{c_{2}\xi}+\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{2c_{2}r}},

where we have also used the mean value theorem, (
3.33
) with
\tau=2R^{2}r
and the fact that
\Phi^{\prime}\geq 0
is non-decreasing.

Step 2.
Construct a suitable barrier function. More precisely, we want to prove that if
C_{0}=2^{10}
and
c_{2}
is sufficiently large, then

(3.36)

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))-\partial_{vv}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))\geq 0\quad\text{in }Q_{r},

where the domain
Q_{r}
is given in (
3.30
).

To do so, we note

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))=\left(\frac{R^{2}}{2r}(x+r)-R(v-v_{0})\right)\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})

and

\displaystyle\partial_{vv}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))=128r\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})+\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})\left(-R(x+r)+128r(v-v_{0})\right)^{2}.

Thus, we get for any
(x,v)\in Q_{r}
,

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))-\partial_{vv}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))\geq\left[vR^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}\right)-128r\right]\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})-c(Rr)^{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})

for some constant
c\geq 1
. We now choose
C_{0}=2^{10}
so that

(3.37)

\left[vR^{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}\right)-128r\right]\geq\left[v_{0}R^{2}\left(1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}\right)-128r\right]\geq\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{4}-128r\geq\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{8},

which implies,

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))-\partial_{vv}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))\geq\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{8}\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})-c(Rr)^{2}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2}),

in
Q_{r}
for some constant
c
. Indeed, we have used the fact that
v_{0}\geq R
.
Moreover, we get

(3.38)

\displaystyle\frac{R^{2}r}{16}\leq R^{2}(x+r)\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R(x+r)\left(\frac{R(x+r)}{4r}-(v-v_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{2}(x,v)\leq\rho^{2}\left(r,v_{0}-\sqrt{2}R/8\right)\leq 4rR^{2},

i.e. it holds
r\eqsim R^{-2}\rho^{2}
in
Q_{r}
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))-\partial_{vv}(\Phi(\rho^{2}))\geq\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{8}\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})-c\rho^{2}r\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})\geq cR^{-2}\left(\frac{v_{0}R^{4}}{8c}\Phi^{\prime}(\rho^{2})-\rho^{4}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\rho^{2})\right).

We now take
c_{2}=8c\geq 1
and use (
3.32
) to deduce (
3.36
).

Step 3.
We now want to prove for any
r\leq R^{3}/2^{10}
, we have

(3.39)

\displaystyle h(x,v)\leq\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\Psi(\rho^{2})\quad\text{in }Q_{r}.

To this end, we note from (
3.28
) and (
3.34
) that

\displaystyle\Psi(\rho^{2})\geq 1\quad\text{on }[0,r]\times\left\{v=v_{0}\pm\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R\right\},

and

\displaystyle\Psi(\rho^{2})\geq 0\quad\text{on }\{x=0\}\times\left[v_{0}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R,v_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}R\right],

as the function
\Phi\geq 0
given in (
3.31
) is non-decreasing.
Using these observations and (
3.36
), we obtain

\left\{\begin{aligned} v\partial_{x}(\Psi(\rho^{2}))-\partial_{vv}(\Psi(\rho^{2}))&\geq 0&&\qquad\mbox{in $Q_{r}$},\\
\Psi(\rho^{2})&\geq\frac{h}{\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}Q_{r}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

By the maximum principle, we derive (
3.39
) for any
r\leq R^{3}/2^{10}
. Due to (
3.39
), (
3.35
), we get (
3.29
).

Finally, for any
r\leq R^{3}/2^{10}
and
C_{1}=4c_{2}
together with the fact that
\rho^{2}(r,v_{0})=R^{2}r
, where the constant
c_{2}\geq 1
is determined in
Step 2
, we use (
3.29
) to get

\displaystyle h(r,v_{0})\leq\left(\frac{v_{0}}{4c_{2}r^{2}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}r}\right)R^{2}re^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{2c_{2}r}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q_{r})}\leq\left(\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{4c_{2}r}+1\right)e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{2c_{2}r}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq 2e^{-\frac{v_{0}R^{2}}{C_{1}r}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)},

where we have used the fact that
\frac{\xi}{2}e^{-\xi}\leq e^{-\frac{\xi}{2}}
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that we can establish the same estimate for
(-h)
. Altogether, by choosing
x=r
, we derive (
3.27
) whenever
x\leq R^{3}/2^{10}
.
∎

Now, we are in a position to prove boundedness of the function
h/\phi
at
(0,0)
, where
h
is a solution to (
3.19
). Here, the key difficulty is that
\phi
vanishes exponentially near
\gamma_{-}
, whereas
subsection 3.2
yields exponential decay at a rate that is suboptimal for large
v>0
. We circumvent this issue by application of
subsection 3.2
on a suitably small scale and introducing another barrier function.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
h
be a weak solution to (
3.19
). Then there are constants
R_{0},c\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle|h/\phi|\leq c\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{{1}})}\quad\text{in }H_{1/R_{0}}.

Proof.

First, fix a constant
c\geq 1
, which will be determined later (see (
3.43
)) and define

(3.40)

\displaystyle\psi(x,v)\coloneqq c\phi(x,v)+2ve^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}},

where the constant
C_{1}
is given in
subsection 3.2
. Then we observe

(3.41)

\displaystyle v\partial_{x}\psi-\partial_{vv}\psi\geq v^{2}\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\geq 0,

and

(3.42)

\displaystyle\psi(x,v)\geq e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\quad\text{on }[0,2^{-13}]\times\{v=1/2\}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi(0,v)=0\quad\text{for }v\in(0,1/2].

Moreover, we get by taking
c\geq 1
sufficiently large,

(3.43)

\displaystyle\psi(x,v)\geq 1\quad\text{on }([2^{-13},1/8]\times\{v=1/2\})\cup(\{x=1/8\}\times[-1/2,0])\cup([0,1/8]\times\{v=-1/2\}),

where we have used (
C.36
).
Now note from
subsection 3.2
with
\mathcal{R}=1
,
v_{0}=1/2
, and
R=v_{0}
that

(3.44)

\displaystyle h(x,1/2)\leq e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\quad\text{for any }x\in[0,2^{-13}].

Thus by (
3.44
), (
3.42
), and (
3.43
), we have

(3.45)

\displaystyle\psi\geq\frac{h}{\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}}\quad\text{on }\partial_{\mathrm{kin}}H_{1/2}.

Thus, by applying maximum principle to
\psi
and
h
, which is justified by (
3.41
) and (
3.45
), we deduce

(3.46)

\displaystyle h\leq\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\psi\quad\text{in }H_{1/2}.

We are now going to prove that when
R_{0}\geq 2
is sufficiently large, then

(3.47)

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\leq c\phi(x,v)\quad\text{in }H_{1/R_{0}}.

To this end, note that there is a sufficiently large
R_{0}\geq 1
such that

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\leq xR_{0}^{\frac{5}{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{9R_{0}^{3}x}}\leq xv^{-\frac{5}{2}}e^{-\frac{v^{3}}{9x}}\quad\text{for any }(x,v)\in H_{1/R_{0}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{-},

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\leq x^{\frac{1}{6}}\quad\text{for any }(x,v)\in H_{1/R_{0}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{0},

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{C_{1}}\frac{1}{8x}}\leq|v|^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{for any }(x,v)\in H_{1/R_{0}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{+}.

Hence, we deduce (
3.47
) from (
C.36
). By (
3.47
) along with (
3.40
), we further estimate (
3.46
) by

(3.48)

\displaystyle h\leq c\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\phi\quad\text{in }H_{1/R_{0}}

for some constant
c\geq 1
. Similarly, we prove

(3.49)

\displaystyle(-h)\leq c\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}\phi\quad\text{in }H_{1/R_{0}}

by considering the function
(-h)
instead of
h
. Since
\phi
is nonnegative, a combination of (
3.48
) and (
3.49
) yields the desired estimate.
∎

We are now ready to prove
subsection 3.2
.

Proof of
subsection 3.2
.

First, note that it suffices to prove the result on scale one, namely that any weak solution
h
to (
3.19
) satisfies

(3.50)

\displaystyle\sup_{H_{r}}\frac{h}{\phi}-\inf_{H_{r}}\frac{h}{\phi}\leq cr^{\alpha}\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}

for any
r\leq 1/R_{0}
, where
c,R_{0}\geq 1
are determined by
subsection 3.2
.
Indeed, by taking
h_{R}(x,v)\coloneqq h(R^{3}x,Rv)
and
\phi_{R}(x,v)\coloneqq\phi(R^{3}x,Rv)/R^{\frac{1}{2}}
to ensure that
\phi_{R}(x,v)=\phi(x,v)
, we have (
3.20
) and (
3.25
) with
h
and
\phi
replaced by
h_{R}
and
\phi_{R}
, respectively. By scaling back, we get the desired estimates Moreover, (
3.17
) follows by rescaling
subsection 3.2
.

We prove the estimate (
3.50
) on scale one by an induction argument based on
subsection 3.2
,
subsection 3.2
, and
subsection 3.2
. More precisely, we find constants
M_{i}
,
m_{i}
,
K\geq 1
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle m_{i}\leq\frac{h}{\phi}\leq M_{i}\quad\text{in }H_{\mathcal{R}_{i}},

and

(3.51)

\displaystyle M_{i}-m_{i}={K\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}}{\mathcal{R}_{i}^{\alpha}},

where
\mathcal{R}_{i}\coloneqq(1/R_{0})^{i+1}
. Moreover, we may assume
R_{0}\geq\frac{8}{\theta}
, where the constant
\theta\in(0,1/2)
is given in
subsection 3.2
.
By
subsection 3.2
, we have
|h/\phi|\leq c\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(H_{1})}
in
